Mosca's inequality (x + y > z): x = 10, y = 5, z = 8 → migration would finish too late under this scenario. z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

Post-quantum maturity: Level 2 of 4 — post-quantum in use outside the critical paths. Next step: Extend post-quantum protection to the critical roles that stay classical, starting with what can be recorded today (wrapped keys, channel key exchange).

Scope and boundaries

Mode: Document audit

Repository: https://github.com/algorand/go-algorand @ e46aea5466a1152af6e08f83555ed16cfcd89f49 — evidence links open the lines at this commit.

Supplied inputs

1160 files, 10256158 bytes in total, 1 redaction(s); the per-file SHA-256 list is in appendix C.

Methodology

The audit was performed by the QSI engine 1.3.0. Each analyzer emits facts (rule, parameters, evidence); finding texts are generated from the rule catalog. Methodology sources are listed under "Sources and normative documents".

Prioritization uses an explainable weighted model (severity, exploitability, exposure, confidentiality/integrity/availability impact weighted by the "Crypto exchange, custodian or wallet" profile, asset criticality, urgency, ease of remediation) with a confidence multiplier. CVSS v3.1 is applied only to technical vulnerabilities with a defined vector. Long-term quantum risks use Mosca's inequality; their severity is capped at Medium and they are never presented as exploitable vulnerabilities.

Finding classes: confirmed technical vulnerability; known vulnerable component (a dependency version named in an advisory; whether the affected code is reachable is assessed separately, and a component confirmed by review becomes a vulnerability); confirmed configuration error; potential cryptographic risk; long-term post-quantum risk; improvement recommendation; insufficient data. Heuristic findings are marked preliminary; analyst decisions are shown next to the findings.

Architecture overview

Files no analyzer reads include: .aptly.conf, .claude/skills/combine-dependabot-prs/SKILL.md, .claude/skills/promote-vfuture/SKILL.md, .claude/skills/spec-consensus-param/SKILL.md, .codecov.yml. 64 files in build-output or dependency-cache directories (.git, .hg, .venv, __pycache__, build, dist, node_modules, target, vendor, venv) were not read. No requests were sent to the owner's systems.

CVSS v3.1
not applicable
Mosca's inequality (x + y > z)
x=10.0, y=5.0, z=8.0 → migration would finish too late under this scenario

Potential impact

Long-term risk once a CRQC exists; for key exchange, recorded traffic could be decrypted retroactively.

Reproduction steps

Open line 1 in the supplied file installer/rpm/RPM-GPG-KEY-Algorand and inspect the quoted lines (see evidence).

Evidence

key · installer/rpm/RPM-GPG-KEY-Algorand · line 1 · SHA-256 99f8a76d7955bc1c… · File da73bea9d17be736…

key · installer/rpm/RPM-GPG-KEY-Algorand · line 1 · SHA-256 a744425426415d58… · File da73bea9d17be736…
OpenPGP subkey: algorithm ID 1 (RSA) 2048-bit

Limitations of the check

This is a forward-looking risk that depends on the arrival of a cryptographically relevant quantum computer (CRQC). No such computer is publicly known to exist today; the timeline is uncertain. It is not a present-day exploitable vulnerability.

Recommendation

Inventory and plan migration to ML-DSA (FIPS 204) or SLH-DSA (FIPS 205), in a hybrid/composite form where the ecosystem supports it..

Retest procedure

Re-audit after migration.

P. W. Shor — Polynomial-Time Algorithms for Prime Factorization and Discrete Logarithms on a Quantum Computer (SIAM J. Comput., 1997); NIST IR 8547 — Transition to Post-Quantum Cryptography Standards

QSI-2026-19627 · CODE-QV-ASYMMETRICMedium

Quantum-vulnerable X25519: key wrapping and data encryption at rest (1 file(s))

Long-term post-quantum riskConfidence: Mediumpreliminary — requires further verification

1 reference(s) to X25519 across 1 file(s), e.g. crypto/libsodium-fork/src/libsodium/crypto_box/crypto_box_seal.c:32. Role, judged from paths, function names and nearby code: key wrapping and data encryption at rest. Keys or data encrypted to these public keys are stored: anyone who copies them now decrypts them once a CRQC exists (harvest now, decrypt later).

Affected asset
source-code:X25519:key_wrapping
Data source
crypto/libsodium-fork/src/libsodium/crypto_box/crypto_box_seal.c
Detection method
Regular-expression matching of crypto API patterns; the role comes from words in paths, enclosing function names and the lines around the matches.
Analyzer
source_code 1.3.0 · 2026-11-06 16:16 UTC
Priority score
36.9 / 100 (Crypto exchange, custodian or wallet)
CVSS v3.1
not applicable
Mosca's inequality (x + y > z)
x=10.0, y=5.0, z=8.0 → migration would finish too late under this scenario

Potential impact

Long-term risk depends on what the algorithm protects: signatures (authenticity until migration) or key exchange/encryption (retroactive decryption of recorded data).

Reproduction steps

Open crypto/libsodium-fork/src/libsodium/crypto_box/crypto_box_seal.c:32 and inspect the quoted lines; the evidence lists further files.

Evidence

   33 |         return -1; /* LCOV_EXCL_LINE */

Limitations of the check

This is a forward-looking risk that depends on the arrival of a cryptographically relevant quantum computer (CRQC). No such computer is publicly known to exist today; the timeline is uncertain. It is not a present-day exploitable vulnerability. Heuristic pattern match on source text without data-flow analysis. The usage context (security-relevant or not) must be confirmed manually; treat as preliminary.

Recommendation

Wrap keys with ML-KEM or a hybrid KEM (X25519 + ML-KEM-768) and re-wrap the stored keys; data that may already have been copied needs its own assessment.

Retest procedure

Re-run the inventory after refactoring.

P. W. Shor — Polynomial-Time Algorithms for Prime Factorization and Discrete Logarithms on a Quantum Computer (SIAM J. Long-term post-quantum riskConfidence: Mediumpreliminary — requires further verification

96 reference(s) to ECDSA across 26 file(s), e.g. crypto/secp256k1/curve.go:33. Role, judged from paths, function names and nearby code: transaction and message signing. Known vulnerable componentConfidence: Lowpreliminary — requires further verification

The advisory carries neither a CVSS vector nor a database severity, so QSI rates it low and preliminary until it is triaged. The advisory names no affected functions, so reachability was not determined.

Evidence

tool_output · OSV.dev GO-2024-3218 · line 39 · SHA-256 78f9da1b356c08be…
{"id": "GO-2024-3218", "aliases": ["CVE-2023-26248", "GHSA-mqr9-hjr8-2m9w"], "modified": "2026-07-01T14:59:24.616695200Z", "summary": "Content Censorship in the InterPlanetary File System (IPFS) via Kademlia DHT abuse in github.com/libp2p/go-libp2p-kad-dht", "severity": null, "declared_in_manifest": true}

Limitations of the check

Advisory data comes from OSV.dev at the recorded time. The advisory has no severity rating. Reachability is judged from names, not a call graph: the affected functions the advisory lists against the identifiers in the audited product code, a Cargo feature it says is off by default against the audited manifests, the platforms it lists against the declared Rust targets. Indirect calls (traits, macros, other dependencies) are not followed. A Go function counts only in a file that also names its package (a method: its type); unexported functions only through the exported ones.

Recommendation

Post-quantum assessment

Key establishment (x = how long data must stay confidential; harvest now, decrypt later)Signatures (x = how long signatures/keys must stay trusted)
Data/key lifetime x1010
Migration time y55
Assumed years to CRQC z8 (early)8
x + y − z7 → migration would finish too late under this scenario7 → migration would finish too late under this scenario

Planning assumptions: x for key establishment, x for signatures, y are the defaults of the risk profile “Crypto exchange, custodian or wallet”, not the client's figures; with the client's actual lifetimes the verdict can change.

Post-quantum maturity: Level 2 of 4 — post-quantum in use outside the critical paths

  • In use: FN-DSA — data/transactions/logic/doc.go
  • In use: FN-DSA — data/transactions/logic/opcodes.go
  • Present, not wired: FN-DSA — crypto/falconWrapper.go
  • Classical only — key wrapping and data encryption at rest: X25519 (1 file(s))

Next step: Extend post-quantum protection to the critical roles that stay classical, starting with what can be recorded today (wrapped keys, channel key exchange).

Levels: 0 none; 1 present, not wired; 2 in use outside the critical paths; 3 hybrid in a critical path; 4 post-quantum by default in the critical paths (root of trust, key wrapping, channel key exchange). Judged from code and manifests.

What the quantum-vulnerable algorithms do

RoleAlgorithms (files)IDFirst step
key wrapping and data encryption at restX25519 (1)QSI-2026-19627Wrap keys with ML-KEM or a hybrid KEM (X25519 + ML-KEM-768) and re-wrap the stored keys; data that may already have been copied needs its own assessment
transaction and message signingECDSA (26)QSI-2026-19628Follow each chain's post-quantum roadmap, avoid public-key reuse, and keep signers ready to add post-quantum signature schemes as chains adopt them
role not determinedEd25519 (19), X25519 (12), ECDH (4), RSA (1)QSI-2026-19625, QSI-2026-19626, QSI-2026-19629, QSI-2026-19632Classify each usage, record data lifetimes, and plan migration to ML-DSA (FIPS 204) or SLH-DSA (FIPS 205), in a hybrid/composite form where the ecosystem supports it; introduce an abstraction layer for crypto-agility

Roles are judged from file paths, function names and the code around each match; review confirms them. Root-of-trust keys and wrapped keys come first: they are hard to replace or can be recorded today.

z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

Quantum algorithms relevant to this inventory

Quantum algorithmEffectInventoried algorithms (rows)What it means for this audit
Shor's algorithm — factoring
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		breaks RSA (3)Breaks RSA at every key length: a longer modulus only raises the cost polynomially. Plan migration to ML-KEM (or a hybrid) for key establishment and ML-DSA / SLH-DSA for signatures; start with keys protecting data that must stay confidential for years (harvest now, decrypt later) and with long-lived signatures.
Shor's algorithm — elliptic-curve discrete logarithms
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		breaks ECDH (4), ECDSA (41), Ed25519 (32), X25519 (22)Elliptic-curve keys are expected to fall before RSA keys of comparable classical strength. On blockchains, coins whose public key is already on chain (reused addresses, pay-to-public-key and Taproot outputs) are exposed first; plan the move to post-quantum signatures and avoid key reuse in the meantime.
Grover's algorithm — key search and preimages
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		weakens (larger keys restore the margin) AES-256 (3), Argon2 (21), BLAKE2 (33), ChaCha20-Poly1305 (28), HKDF (1), HMAC (6), Keccak-256 (2), PBKDF2 (2), Poly1305 (21), Poseidon (5), SHA-2 (25), SHA-3 (2), SHAKE (1), XSalsa20-Poly1305 (43), scrypt (4)Not a break. 256-bit keys (AES-256, ChaCha20) keep a comfortable margin; AES-128 meets NIST category 1, while some authorities ask for 256-bit keys in quantum-resistant contexts (see the AES-128 entry). Prefer 256-bit keys for long-lived data; 256-bit hashes need no change for preimage resistance.
Brassard–Høyer–Tapp — collision search
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		weakens (larger keys restore the margin) BLAKE2 (33), Keccak-256 (2), Poseidon (5), SHA-2 (25), SHA-3 (2), SHAKE (1)256-bit hashes (SHA-256, SHA3-256) remain adequate for collision resistance; 160-bit hashes are already weak classically (SHA-1 collisions are practical). Use 384/512-bit hashes where NIST categories 4–5 are required.
Quantum lattice sieving
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		included in standard parameters FN-DSA (3)No change for ML-KEM or ML-DSA at the standard parameter sets. A 2024 claim of a polynomial-time quantum LWE algorithm was withdrawn after a bug was found (see claims).

Effects: breaks — replace the algorithm; weakens — larger keys or outputs restore the margin; included in standard parameters — no action at standard parameter sets; only with superposition queries — not applicable to ordinary deployments.

Limitations and assumptions

  • This report presents automated and documented analysis results for the stated scope. It is not a legal opinion, a certification or an attestation of compliance with any standard.
  • z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

None (documentation and design work).
Tests before change
Confirm the inventory entry matches the deployed configuration.
Tests after change

Closure criteria
Inventory entry with owner, lifetime and target algorithm exists.

QSI-2026-19630 · P4 · 90 daysroot-cause fix

Plan migration of RSA to ML-DSA (FIPS 204) or SLH-DSA (FIPS 205), in a hybrid/composite form where the ecosystem supports it. (or a hybrid scheme) once supported by your libraries, protocols and counterparties.

Rationale
Removes dependence on a quantum-vulnerable public-key primitive.
Expected outcome
The asset uses a NIST-standardized post-quantum or hybrid algorithm.
Complexity
high
Dependencies
Library/protocol support (e.g. OpenSSL ≥ 3.5, HSM firmware), interoperability with peers.
Side effects
Larger keys/signatures, possible performance and compatibility impact.
Tests before change
Interoperability tests in a staging environment.
Tests after change
Verify the negotiated/used algorithm on the production asset.
Closure criteria
Re-audit shows ML-DSA (FIPS 204) or SLH-DSA (FIPS 205), in a hybrid/composite form where the ecosystem supports it. or a hybrid scheme in use.

Sources and normative documents

Reviewed: 2026-10-13

  1. A. Becker, L. Ducas, N. Gama, T. Laarhoven — New directions in nearest neighbor searching with applications to lattice sieving (SODA 2016; IACR ePrint 2015/1128) — IACR ePrint. https://eprint.iacr.org/2015/1128 (peer-reviewed article: classical sieving in 2^{0.292n + o(n)})
  2. A. Chailloux, J. Loyer — Lattice sieving via quantum random walks (ASIACRYPT 2021; IACR ePrint 2021/570) — IACR ePrint. https://eprint.iacr.org/2021/570 (peer-reviewed article: 2^{0.2570d + o(d)}, previously 2^{0.2653d + o(d)} (Laarhoven))
  3. A. Chailloux, M. Naya-Plasencia, A. Schrottenloher — An Efficient Quantum Collision Search Algorithm and Implications on Symmetric Cryptography (ASIACRYPT 2017; IACR ePrint 2017/847) — IACR ePrint. https://eprint.iacr.org/2017/847 (peer-reviewed article: Õ(2^{2n/5}) time, O(n) qubits, Õ(2^{n/5}) classical memory)
  4. C. Chevignard, P.-A. Fouque, A. Schrottenloher — Reducing the Number of Qubits in Quantum Discrete Logarithms on Elliptic Curves (IACR ePrint 2026/280) — IACR ePrint. https://eprint.iacr.org/2026/280 (revision of a EUROCRYPT 2026 paper: 3.12n + o(n) qubits; for n = 256, 1193 qubits and 22 runs of 2^38.98 Toffoli gates)
  5. C. Gidney — How to factor 2048 bit RSA integers with less than a million noisy qubits (arXiv:2505.15917) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15917 (preprint (2025-05-21): < 1 million noisy qubits, < 1 week, 0.1% gate error, 1 µs surface-code cycle)
  6. C. Gidney, M. Ekerå — How to factor 2048 bit RSA integers in 8 hours using 20 million noisy qubits (Quantum 5, 433) — Quantum. https://doi.org/10.22331/q-2021-04-15-433 (peer-reviewed article (2021): 10^-3 gate error, 1 µs cycle, 10 µs reaction time)
  7. C. Zalka — Grover's quantum searching algorithm is optimal (Phys. Rev. A 60, 2746, 1999; arXiv:quant-ph/9711070) — APS. https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevA.60.2746 (peer-reviewed article: optimal for any success probability; parallel search gains no more than splitting the space)
  8. CRYSTALS-Kyber — Algorithm Specifications and Supporting Documentation (round 3, 2021-08-04) — CRYSTALS team. https://pq-crystals.org/kyber/data/kyber-specification-round3-20210804.pdf (NIST round-3 submission: core-SVP cost 2^{0.292β} classical, 2^{0.265β} quantum; Kyber768 166 quantum bits)
  9. Common Vulnerability Scoring System v3.1: Specification Document — FIRST. https://www.first.org/cvss/v3.1/specification-document (final)
  10. D. J. Bernstein — Cost analysis of hash collisions: Will quantum computers make SHARCS obsolete? (2009) — cr.yp.to. https://cr.yp.to/hash/collisioncost-20090823.pdf (paper dated 2009-08-23)
  11. D. Litinski — How to compute a 256-bit elliptic curve private key with only 50 million Toffoli gates (arXiv:2306.08585) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.08585 (preprint (2023-06-14): 6000 modules × 1152 physical qubits, one key per 10 minutes under its architecture assumptions)
  12. FIPS 203 — Module-Lattice-Based Key-Encapsulation Mechanism Standard (ML-KEM) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/203/final (final (August 2024))
  13. FIPS 204 — Module-Lattice-Based Digital Signature Standard (ML-DSA) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/204/final (final (August 2024))
  14. FIPS 205 — Stateless Hash-Based Digital Signature Standard (SLH-DSA) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/205/final (final (August 2024))
  15. G. Brassard, P. Høyer, A. Tapp — Quantum Algorithm for the Collision Problem (LATIN '98; arXiv:quant-ph/9705002) — Springer / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/quant-ph/9705002 (peer-reviewed article: collisions of r-to-one functions in O((N/r)^{1/3}) expected evaluations)
  16. J. Proos, C. Zalka — Shor's discrete logarithm quantum algorithm for elliptic curves (Quantum Inf. Comput. 3(4), 2003; arXiv:quant-ph/0301141) — Quantum Information & Computation. https://doi.org/10.26421/QIC3.4-3 (peer-reviewed article)
  17. L. K. Grover — A fast quantum mechanical algorithm for database search (STOC 1996) — ACM. https://doi.org/10.1145/237814.237866 (peer-reviewed article)
  18. M. Amy et al. — Estimating the cost of generic quantum pre-image attacks on SHA-2 and SHA-3 (SAC 2016; arXiv:1603.09383) — Springer / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/1603.09383 (peer-reviewed article: SHA-256 about 2^153.8 surface-code cycles on 2^12.6 logical qubits; SHA3-256 about 2^146.5 cycles on 2^20)
  19. M. Cain et al. — Shor's algorithm is possible with as few as 10,000 reconfigurable atomic qubits (arXiv:2603.28627) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28627 (preprint (2026-03-30): P-256 in a few days with 26,000 physical neutral-atom qubits; RSA-2048 one to two orders of magnitude longer)
  20. M. Mosca — Cybersecurity in an Era with Quantum Computers: Will We Be Ready? (IEEE Security & Privacy, 2018) — IEEE. https://doi.org/10.1109/MSP.2018.3761723 (peer-reviewed article; source of the x + y > z migration inequality)
  21. M. Roetteler, M. Naehrig, K. Svore, K. Lauter — Quantum resource estimates for computing elliptic curve discrete logarithms (ASIACRYPT 2017) — IACR / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.06752 (peer-reviewed article: at most 9n + 2⌈log2 n⌉ + 10 qubits and 448 n^3 log2 n + 4090 n^3 Toffoli gates)
  22. NIST IR 8547 — Transition to Post-Quantum Cryptography Standards — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/ir/8547/final (final (2026-10-27; the initial public draft of November 2024 is obsoleted). Tables 2 and 4: ECDSA, EdDSA and RSA signatures, finite-field and elliptic-curve DH and MQV, and RSA key establishment are deprecated after 2030 and disallowed after 2035 at every key size — the draft deprecated only the 112-bit level. A composite (hybrid) key establishment stays FIPS 140-validatable while one input comes from an approved scheme. Repeats the federal deadlines of OMB M-26-15: post-quantum key establishment by 2030-12-31 and signatures by 2031-12-31 for high value assets and high impact systems)
  23. NIST — Post-Quantum Cryptography Standardization: Evaluation Criteria, Security (Call for Proposals, Section 4.A.5) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/Projects/Post-Quantum-Cryptography/Post-Quantum-Cryptography-Standardization/Evaluation-Criteria/Security-(Evaluation-Criteria) (security categories: AES-128 key search 2^170/MAXDEPTH quantum gates, AES-192 2^233/MAXDEPTH, AES-256 2^298/MAXDEPTH; MAXDEPTH 2^40–2^96)
  24. OSV advisory GHSA-246p-cpwv-v3jq — OSV.dev. https://osv.dev/vulnerability/GHSA-246p-cpwv-v3jq (modified 2026-11-05T23:26:14.796992232Z)
  25. OSV advisory GHSA-259r-337f-4rfw — OSV.dev. https://osv.dev/vulnerability/GHSA-259r-337f-4rfw (modified 2026-10-20T14:45:03.782718646Z)
